348 — Incremental Build Graph Engine

Goal: Implement, from scratch in Rust, the core of an incremental build system: a build graph (DAG of source/action/glob nodes with declared inputs/outputs), fine-grained change detection (blake3 content fingerprints + stat fast-path + action-key hashing), a demand-driven incremental evaluator with Salsa-style red/green revision counters and early cutoff, a content-addressed action cache + CAS output store, a parallel work-stealing scheduler with critical-path ordering, dynamic dependencies, file-watch invalidation, crash-consistent cross-invocation state persistence, hermetic sandboxed execution with undeclared-input detection, and a remote-cache/execution interface (crates ibg-core/graph/fingerprint/engine/cache/exec/sched/watch/persist/sandbox/remote). It is the language-agnostic engine modeling the Bazel / Buck2 / Salsa / Shake family with actions as abstract command + declared inputs/outputs — not a specific compiler, package manager, or CI system. Validated by differential comparison against from-scratch full rebuilds for correctness (no under- or over-build) and large-graph incremental-edit benchmarks.

Project Number: 348

Project Name: Incremental Build Graph Engine

Owner: Mujir Qureshi

Document: FINAL_BLUEPRINT.md

Status: specification — locked

1. Goal

1.1 Engineering Definition

Build a language-agnostic, demand-driven incremental build graph engine — the core evaluation substrate for a Bazel/Buck2/Salsa-class build system. Deliverable: a family of Rust crates that model a build DAG, detect fine-grained changes via content fingerprints, evaluate nodes on demand with early cutoff, cache action results in a content-addressed store, schedule parallel work via work-stealing, persist state crash-consistently across invocations, and optionally sandbox actions for hermeticity. The engine exposes a graph-construction API; it is NOT a build-file DSL parser, NOT a language-specific compiler, NOT a package/dependency manager (→ project 339), NOT a CI orchestrator.

1.2 System Boundaries

Inside Outside
DAG of source/action/glob/group nodes Starlark / BUILD-file front-end
Content fingerprinting (blake3) + stat fast-path Language-specific compiler logic
Demand-driven pull eval + early cutoff Package resolution / semver (→ project 339)
ActionKey-based action cache (local + remote interface) CI job dispatch / artifact publishing
Work-stealing scheduler + critical-path ordering IDE language server protocol
Crash-consistent incremental state (ibg-persist) Build-rule standard library
File-watch invalidation via reverse edges Remote execution worker fleet management
Hermetic sandbox + undeclared-input detection Distributed cache backend implementation
Remote cache/exec protocol boundary (REAPI interface) Starlark eval, query language

1.3 Non-Goals

  • No Starlark/Python/Jsonnet BUILD-file parser (hook point provided; DSL is out-of-core).
  • No language-specific compilation: no rustc, javac, clang integration beyond command: Cmd abstraction.
  • No package-manager dependency resolution (that is project 339).
  • No CI scheduling, test sharding, artifact retention policies.
  • No remote execution worker: REAPI client interface only; fleet management out-of-scope.
  • No distributed graph partitioning across machines (single-host engine; remote cache/exec is pluggable backend).
  • No build-rule versioning, migration tooling, or rule-authoring IDE support.

1.4 Intended Deployment Model

  • Embedded library: integrated by a build-tool front-end (CLI or daemon) that constructs the graph.
  • Daemon mode: long-running process; ibg-watch feeds invalidation events; clients connect via local IPC (Unix socket).
  • Single-host: all scheduling/execution on one machine; remote cache/exec optional via ibg-remote.
  • Workspace root: one engine instance per workspace root; multiple targets per invocation.
  • State persistence: ibg-persist writes to a .ibg/ directory at workspace root.

1.5 Target Users / Operators / Developers

Role Touchpoint
Build-tool author ibg-graph + ibg-engine API; constructs DAG programmatically
Build-file DSL integrator Graph-construction API + hook callbacks
DevOps / build-infra engineer ibg-cli, ibg-telemetry, remote-cache backend config
Portfolio engineer (this project) All crates; systems-level understanding of incrementality
Reviewer / interviewer ADRs, invariants, threat model, failure-mode tables

1.6 Phase-0 Deliverable — Crate Manifest

Ordered by dependency depth (leaves first):

  1. ibg-core — canonical types: NodeId, NodeKey, Digest, Revision, BuildNode, NodeKind, Action, ActionKey, Edge, NodeState, BuildRequest, BuildResult, IbgError.
  2. ibg-graph — DAG construction, source/action/glob nodes, declared inputs/outputs, cycle detection, topo + reverse-dep index.
  3. ibg-fingerprint — blake3 file digests, stat fast-path, ActionKey computation, digest cache, dirtiness checks.
  4. ibg-engine — demand-driven pull evaluator, memoization, dirty/verified state machine, Revision counters (red/green), early cutoff.
  5. ibg-cache — content-addressed action cache (ActionKey → CacheEntry), CAS output store, local backend, GC/eviction.
  6. ibg-exec — action executor: spawn Cmd, declared-output capture, working-dir/env control, CAS materialization.
  7. ibg-sched — work-stealing scheduler, dependency-aware ready-queue, job-slot limits, critical-path ordering, backpressure.
  8. ibg-watch — fs-event watcher, polling fallback, debounce, dirty propagation through reverse edges.
  9. ibg-persist — durable incremental state DB, crash-consistent writes, schema versioning, migration.
  10. ibg-sandbox — hermetic execution wrapper, undeclared-input detection (strace/Linux namespaces/macOS sandbox), declared-output enforcement.
  11. ibg-remote — REAPI client, Merkle input-tree construction, content-addressed remote upload/download, pluggable backend.
  12. ibg-telemetry — build metrics (cache-hit rate, critical path, recompute count), OpenTelemetry trace export, flamegraph hooks.
  13. ibg-cli — binary driver: graph load, invocation dispatch, daemon control, watch mode.
  14. ibg-bench — benchmark harness: large-graph construction, incremental-edit scenarios, differential correctness, regression tracking.

1.7 Phase Unlock Sequence

Phase Unlocks
0 (core + graph + fingerprint) Static DAG, correctness of change detection
1 (engine + cache) Incremental eval, early cutoff, action-cache hits
2 (exec + sched) Parallel execution, work-stealing, job-slot limits
3 (persist + watch) Cross-invocation state, file-watch daemon mode
4 (sandbox) Hermetic actions, undeclared-input detection
5 (remote) Distributed cache, remote execution protocol
6 (telemetry + bench) Observability, regression baselines, perf tuning

1.10 Language Rationale

Rust (edition 2021, MSRV 1.78).

  • Graph algorithms over large DAGs: arena-allocated node pools, zero-copy adjacency; no GC pauses on hot eval path.
  • blake3 hashing: SIMD-accelerated, Rust-native crate; no FFI overhead.
  • Work-stealing scheduler: rayon / custom deque; ownership model prevents data races on node state.
  • mmap-backed caches and persist store: memmap2; Rust lifetime system enforces unmapping discipline.
  • Crash-consistent writes: POSIX fsync/rename sequences; Rust's io::Error forces explicit handling.
  • async + tokio for file-watch integration and remote cache I/O; sync rayon for CPU-bound graph traversal.
  • Rejected: Go (GC + weaker struct-layout control; no arena alloc; false sharing on goroutine stacks). Haskell (Shake precedent; GC pauses; lazy I/O unsound for FS state); OCaml (similar GC concern; weak SIMD). Both cited as academic references.

1.11 Prerequisites

  • Systems: OS process model, fork/exec, filesystem stat/inode, inotify/kqueue, POSIX mmap, fsync, directory traversal.
  • Concurrency: work-stealing deques, lock-free queues, Arc/Mutex/RwLock, async Rust (tokio), rayon, memory ordering.
  • Graph algorithms: topological sort (Kahn's algorithm), cycle detection (DFS coloring), reverse-dependency indexing, critical-path BFS.
  • Distributed systems: content-addressed storage, Merkle trees, cache invalidation, idempotent retries, remote execution protocols (REAPI).
  • Storage: write-ahead log patterns, crash consistency, schema versioning, sled / redb / custom log-structured store.
  • Hashing / cryptography: blake3, digest collision resistance, Merkle directory hashing (Bazel spec).

2. Why Build This

2.1 Unique Systems Knowledge Taught

Domain Concrete skill acquired
Incremental computation theory Implementing red/green Revision counters (Salsa model); early-cutoff propagation; demand-driven pull vs push evaluation
Build-systems science "Build Systems à la Carte" rebuilder×scheduler taxonomy; correctness (no under-build) vs minimality (no over-build) duality
Content-addressed storage CAS design: digest → blob; deduplication; GC; Merkle directory trees (Bazel REAPI)
Work-stealing scheduling Lock-free Chase-Lev deque; critical-path heuristic; backpressure; dynamic dependency feedback
Hermetic execution Namespace-based sandboxing (Linux); strace-based syscall audit; declared vs undeclared input enforcement
Crash-consistent persistence Atomic rename pattern; WAL; schema migration; torn-write detection
File-system watching inotify/kqueue semantics; debounce; coalesce; overflow/drop handling; polling fallback
Fingerprinting + hashing blake3 SIMD; stat fast-path (mtime+size+ino); digest cache; hash-of-hashes for ActionKey

2.2 Historical + Architectural Significance

  • Build incrementality is a solved-but-hard problem: every major software org re-solves it (Make → Bazel → Buck2 → Turborepo → Gradle → Pants). The engineering surface is enormous.
  • "Build Systems à la Carte" (ICFP 2018) provides the first formal taxonomy: correctness/minimality, rebuilder/scheduler separation. This project implements those abstractions concretely.
  • Salsa (Rust-analyzer's incremental engine) demonstrates the Revision-counter model at production scale; this project generalizes it to arbitrary build actions (not just query functions).
  • Bazel's Remote Execution API (REAPI) is the de-facto distributed build standard; implementing the client side teaches distributed caching, Merkle trees, and idempotent remote calls.
  • Early cutoff is the key insight separating "minimal rebuild" from "correct rebuild": knowing it eliminates unnecessary fan-out propagation; implementing it correctly requires careful Revision bookkeeping.

2.3 Engineering Muscles Developed

  • DAG lifecycle: construction, validation (cycle check), index maintenance, incremental structural change.
  • State machines under concurrency: NodeState transitions (Clean→Dirty→Running→Clean|Failed) across multiple threads; proof that no node is executed twice simultaneously.
  • Cache correctness: ActionKey collision resistance; cache poisoning defense; GC without serving stale entries.
  • Observability-first design: every subsystem emits spans; telemetry is not bolted on; debugging a cache miss requires knowing which input digest changed and why.
  • Failure-mode reasoning: partial writes, corrupt DB, clock skew on mtime, non-deterministic actions, OOM during exec, network partition to remote cache.
  • API design: graph-construction API must be expressive enough for a DSL layer yet simple enough to reason about correctness; no leaky abstraction.

2.4 Production Engineering Concepts Exercised

Concept Manifestation
No under-build (safety) Transitive input check; stale output detection; sandbox undeclared-input trap
No over-build (efficiency) ActionKey memoization; early cutoff; stat fast-path before full hash
Observability Per-node span, cache-hit/miss counter, critical-path latency, recompute count
Crash consistency Atomic rename; WAL in ibg-persist; fallback to full re-eval on corruption
Hermetic reproducibility Same ActionKey → same outputs anywhere; reproducibility check (two-run comparison)
Horizontal scaling hook Remote cache protocol; Merkle input trees; content-addressed upload
Backpressure Scheduler job-slot limits; ready-queue depth cap; exec spawn throttle
Schema evolution ibg-persist versioned schema; migration path; forward-compat sentinel

2.5 Cross-Portfolio Links

Related project Link
339 — Package/Dependency Manager ibg-engine is the build evaluator; project 339 is the resolver; they compose
Compiler front-ends (various) ibg-exec wraps compiler invocations; early cutoff avoids unnecessary compiles
Distributed storage / KV engines ibg-cache CAS shares design patterns with LSM-based content stores
Work-stealing schedulers ibg-sched mirrors GPU/tensor runtime task schedulers (project 3xx GPU runtime)
Consensus / WAL engines ibg-persist crash-consistency parallels Raft log commit + fsync discipline
Networking / RPC stacks ibg-remote speaks REAPI (gRPC + Protobuf) — same patterns as any RPC client
Observability / tracing ibg-telemetry outputs OpenTelemetry spans — composable with any tracing project
File-system / OS internals ibg-watch + ibg-sandbox exercise deep OS APIs (namespaces, inotify, ptrace)

2.6 Calibration Value

  • Demonstrates mastery of incremental computation, parallel scheduling, content-addressed storage, and hermetic execution — four distinct senior-engineer domains in one project.
  • Directly maps to real engineering roles: build-systems engineer (Google/Meta/Stripe), compiler-infrastructure engineer (rust-analyzer, LLVM), platform-infra engineer (CI/CD tooling).
  • Reviewers can probe any of: correctness proof of early cutoff, ActionKey collision probability, crash-consistency of persist, work-stealing starvation freedom, sandbox escape vectors — each a PhD-adjacent topic with a concrete answer in this codebase.
  • Portfolio differentiator: most engineers can write a Makefile wrapper; few can implement a demand-driven revision-based incremental evaluator with content-addressed caching from scratch.

2.7 What Mastery Looks Like

After building this the engineer holds these exact competencies, each tied to a phase:

  • Content-addressed action model (Phase 1–3): can define a hermetic action as (command, declared inputs, outputs, env, tool digest) and explain why the action key is a pure function of its inputs.
  • DAG construction + topological evaluation (Phase 2–4): can build a million-node dependency graph, detect cycles, and schedule by topological order with critical-path priority.
  • Fingerprinting + early cutoff (Phase 5–6): can implement content hashing (blake3) and the green/red early-cutoff optimization that stops propagation when a derived output is unchanged.
  • Action cache + CAS (Phase 6–7): can build a content-addressed store with ≥ 95% warm-build hit rate and reason about deduplication and storage amplification.
  • Hermetic sandboxed execution (Phase 8): can sandbox an action with namespaces/seccomp (Linux) or Seatbelt (macOS) and detect undeclared inputs that break hermeticity.
  • Incremental recompute discipline (Phase 4–6): can achieve a sub-50ms null build via stat fast-paths and explain why correctness requires fingerprinting, not just timestamps.
  • Remote caching (Phase 12): can speak Bazel REAPI v2 and reason about local-vs-remote cache fallback and RTT budgets.
  • Reproducibility verification (Phase 13–14): can detect non-deterministic actions before they poison the cache, and fuzz the fingerprint/persist deserializers.

3. Architectural Overview

3.1 Canonical Architecture Diagram

Show the original text diagram
    build request (targets)
            |
      [ibg-graph] resolve graph: targets -> actions -> inputs (DAG); cycle check; topo + reverse-dep index
            |
      [ibg-engine] demand-driven eval per node: dirty?  (red/green via Revision counters)
            |   |-- [ibg-fingerprint] input digests changed? (stat fast-path -> content hash) -> recompute ActionKey
            |   |-- EARLY CUTOFF: inputs changed BUT action output digest unchanged -> stop; dependents stay verified
            |
      [ibg-cache] action-cache lookup: ActionKey -> hit? materialize outputs from CAS, skip exec
            |  miss
      [ibg-sched] schedule dirty actions by deps + job slots (work-stealing, critical path)
            |
      [ibg-exec] run action  ( [ibg-sandbox] hermetic: detect undeclared inputs, enforce declared outputs )
            |  output digests -> [ibg-cache] store (+ optional [ibg-remote] upload, Merkle input tree)
            |
      [ibg-persist] save graph + NodeStates + Revisions + digests for the next invocation
      [ibg-watch] fs events -> mark nodes Dirty via reverse edges -> next build re-evaluates only the affected subgraph
      [ibg-telemetry] taps eval / cache / exec / sched

3.2 Subsystem Descriptions

ibg-graph — DAG Model

  • Owns BuildNode arena (slab-allocated, NodeId(u32) index); source, action, glob, group node kinds.
  • Maintains adjacency list (deps) + reverse-dep index (rdeps) for O(1) fan-out lookup.
  • Cycle detection: DFS coloring on every add_edge; returns IbgError::CycleDetected immediately.
  • Topo index: Kahn's algorithm; drives engine evaluation order and scheduler priority.
  • Dynamic edges (Edge::Dynamic) added at runtime; triggers incremental re-topo + cycle re-check.
  • Trust boundary: graph is constructed by the front-end (CLI or DSL layer); ibg-graph validates but does not trust caller to be cycle-free.

ibg-engine — Demand-Driven Evaluator

  • Pull-based: evaluates only the transitive dependencies of requested targets.
  • Per-node NodeState { last_verified: Revision, last_changed: Revision, output_digest, status }.
  • Revision counter: global monotonic Revision(u64); incremented at each build invocation start.
  • Red/green: node is "green" (verified) if last_verified == current_revision; "red" (dirty) otherwise.
  • Memoization: if a node is already green, return its output_digest without re-evaluation.
  • Early cutoff: if inputs changed (ActionKey changed) but exec yields same output_digest → do NOT increment last_changed; dependents remain green.
  • Concurrency boundary: engine coordinates with scheduler via a NodeState lock per node (or epoch-based compare-and-swap); no global lock on the DAG.

ibg-fingerprint — Content Hashing

  • Stat fast-path: compare (mtime, size, inode) to cached values; if unchanged, skip full blake3 read.
  • Full hash: blake3::hash(file_bytes) → Digest([u8; 32]); stored in digest cache keyed by (path, mtime, size).
  • ActionKey computation: blake3(command_bytes || sorted(input_digests) || env_bytes || tool_digest).
  • Glob node: digest of sorted (path, file_digest) pairs matching the pattern.
  • Digest cache: HashMap<(PathBuf, u64 /*mtime*/, u64 /*size*/), Digest>; evicted on file change.

ibg-cache — Action Cache + CAS

  • Action cache: ActionKey → CacheEntry { outputs: Vec<Digest>, metadata: ExecMeta }.
  • CAS store: Digest → bytes; content-addressed, deduplication automatic.
  • Local backend: directory-sharded by first 2 hex bytes of digest; mmap for large blobs.
  • GC: mark-and-sweep; sweep unreferenced CAS objects after action-cache GC; configurable retention window.
  • Cache hit path: fetch CacheEntry, materialize each output from CAS to declared output path → skip exec entirely.
  • Trust invariant: a cache hit is correct IFF all declared inputs were correctly fingerprinted; sandbox enforces this.

ibg-sched — Work-Stealing Scheduler

  • Ready queue: actions whose all deps are green and slot is available.
  • Work-stealing: each worker thread has a local Chase-Lev deque; steals from tail of other queues when idle.
  • Critical-path ordering: nodes annotated with estimated cost (prior exec time from ibg-persist); highest-cost first.
  • Job-slot limits: configurable max_jobs; semaphore-based; resource-tagged actions (RAM, CPU) checked against limits.
  • Backpressure: if exec queue depth > threshold, ibg-engine pauses dispatching new nodes.
  • Concurrency boundary: scheduler communicates with engine via mpsc channels; exec via Arc<Mutex<NodeState>>.

ibg-exec — Action Executor

  • Spawns Command (Tokio async process); captures stdout/stderr; checks exit code.
  • Declared-output capture: after exec, reads each declared output path, computes Digest, stores in CAS.
  • Working-dir isolation: each action runs in a temp directory or sandboxed root.
  • Env control: action's EnvMap used verbatim; no ambient env leakage (hermetic actions strip host env).
  • CAS materialization: on cache hit, writes bytes from CAS to declared output paths with correct permissions.

ibg-sandbox — Hermetic Wrapper

  • Linux: mount namespace + pivot_root; bind-mount only declared inputs; seccomp filter; strace-based open syscall audit.
  • macOS: sandbox-exec profile restricting file access to declared inputs + outputs.
  • Undeclared input: any open/stat of a path outside declared inputs → action fails with IbgError::UndeclaredInput; never silently wrong.
  • Declared-output enforcement: after exec, verify each declared output exists; if missing → IbgError::MissingOutput.
  • Reproducibility check: optional two-run mode; compare output digests of both runs; mismatch → IbgError::NonDeterministic.

ibg-watch — File Watching + Invalidation

  • Uses inotify (Linux) / kqueue (macOS) via notify crate; polling fallback for network filesystems.
  • Debounce: coalesce rapid events within 50 ms window; only emit one invalidation per path.
  • Overflow handling: if event queue overflows → full workspace rescan (conservative; safe).
  • Dirty propagation: on path change, look up path → source node → walk reverse edges → mark all transitive dependents NodeState::Dirty.
  • Trust boundary: watch events come from OS; treated as advisory (engine re-fingerprints before deciding to rebuild).

ibg-persist — Durable State

  • Stores: NodeState per NodeId, Digest per (NodeId, Revision), graph structure snapshot, ActionKey history.
  • Backend: redb (pure Rust ACID embedded DB) or sled; pluggable via trait.
  • Crash consistency: writes via DB transaction; on open, validate last committed revision; corrupt/partial → fallback to full re-eval (never wrong incremental result).
  • Schema versioning: magic header + u32 schema version; migration table; forward-compat sentinel rejects unknown versions.
  • Persistence boundary: written at end of each successful build; read at engine startup.

ibg-remote — Remote Cache / Exec Interface

  • Implements Bazel REAPI client: ContentAddressableStorage + ActionCache + Execution gRPC services.
  • Merkle input tree: recursively hashes input directory tree per REAPI spec; uploads missing blobs.
  • Upload: check-then-upload (FindMissingBlobs → BatchUpdateBlobs / ByteStream).
  • Download: on remote cache hit, stream outputs into local CAS → materialize.
  • Protocol boundary: all remote I/O is async (tokio); timeouts + retry with exponential backoff.
  • Trust: remote cache is trusted for output bytes but NOT for ActionKey validity; engine re-verifies input fingerprints before accepting a remote hit.

ibg-telemetry — Metrics + Tracing

  • OpenTelemetry spans: per-node eval span, per-action exec span, cache-lookup span.
  • Counters: cache_hits, cache_misses, nodes_recomputed, nodes_early_cutoff, nodes_skipped.
  • Critical-path computation: post-build BFS on actual exec times; reports longest chain.
  • Export: OTLP exporter (Jaeger / Tempo compatible); optional JSON flamegraph output.
  • Tap points: ibg-engine emits node lifecycle events; ibg-cache emits hit/miss; ibg-sched emits queue-depth samples.

3.3 Data Flow Summary

Stage Input Output Crate
Graph construction front-end calls BuildNode arena + adj/rdep index ibg-graph
Fingerprinting file paths Digest per source, ActionKey per action ibg-fingerprint
Incremental eval BuildRequest, current Revision per-node green/red decision ibg-engine
Cache lookup ActionKey hit → CacheEntry; miss → schedule ibg-cache
Scheduling dirty action set ordered ready queue ibg-sched
Execution Action + sandbox config output files + Digest per output ibg-exec + ibg-sandbox
Cache store output Digests updated CacheEntry in CAS ibg-cache
Persistence end-of-build NodeStates durable DB record ibg-persist
Invalidation fs events dirty NodeId set ibg-watch
Observability lifecycle events spans, counters, flamegraph ibg-telemetry

3.4 Async / Sync Boundary

  • Sync (rayon): graph traversal, fingerprinting, engine eval loop — CPU-bound; no await.
  • Async (tokio): action execution (process spawn + wait), file-watch event loop, remote cache I/O.
  • Bridge: tokio::task::spawn_blocking wraps sync graph/fingerprint work called from async exec path; rayon pool for bulk graph ops.
  • Channel types: tokio::sync::mpsc for watch→engine events; crossbeam::channel for sched↔exec handoff.

3.5 Trust + Security Boundaries

Boundary Trust assumption Enforcement
Front-end → ibg-graph Untrusted; may provide cycles Cycle detection on every add_edge
ibg-cache (local) Trusted filesystem; tamper possible Digest re-verification on output read
ibg-remote Untrusted network Re-verify output digests after download
ibg-sandbox OS kernel enforces namespace Defense-in-depth: seccomp + mount ns
ibg-persist Trusted local disk CRC/hash header; corrupt → re-eval
ibg-watch OS event queue Advisory only; re-fingerprint before rebuild

4. System Constraints

4.1 Constraints Table

Constraint Target Notes
Max nodes in graph 1 000 000 nodes NodeId(u32) → 4 GiB address space ceiling
Max edges (total) 10 000 000 edges Adjacency vec pre-allocated; ~80 MB at 8 bytes/edge
Graph construction time ≤ 500 ms for 500 k nodes Single-thread Kahn topo; parallel slab alloc
Incremental eval latency (null build) p50 ≤ 10 ms, p99 ≤ 50 ms Stat fast-path; all nodes green; no hashing
Dirty-node fingerprint throughput ≥ 500 MB/s effective blake3 SIMD; OS page cache hot
Action cache lookup latency p50 ≤ 1 ms, p99 ≤ 5 ms Local DB (redb); mmap
Scheduler dispatch latency ≤ 500 µs per node Lock-free deque; no syscall on fast path
Max concurrent actions Configurable; default = logical CPUs Semaphore; default num_cpus::get()
Exec spawn latency ≤ 5 ms (process creation) posix_spawn path; no vfork
Peak heap (graph + engine) ≤ 2 GB for 1 M nodes ~2 KB per node budget; arena alloc
ibg-persist write time (end-of-build) ≤ 200 ms for 100 k dirty nodes Batch DB transaction; async fsync
ibg-persist read time (startup) ≤ 300 ms for 1 M nodes Sequential mmap scan
ibg-watch event-to-dirty latency ≤ 100 ms (debounce 50 ms) inotify/kqueue; no polling default
Remote cache upload throughput ≥ 100 MB/s on 1 Gbps LAN Streaming ByteStream; pipelined blobs
Remote cache RTT budget p99 ≤ 200 ms per lookup Timeout; fallback to local exec on breach
CAS storage amplification ≤ 1.05× (dedup saves ≥ 5%) Content-addressing; no duplication
File descriptor budget ≤ 1024 open fds per build Respect ulimit -n; pool inotify watches
Shutdown / SIGTERM response ≤ 2 s clean shutdown Drain exec queue; flush persist; close fds
Crash-recovery time (corrupt DB) ≤ 10 s to full re-eval fallback Re-fingerprint all sources; skip persist read
RTO (resume from clean state) Full re-eval completes No partial-state poisoning
Availability target (daemon mode) 99.9% uptime (in-process) Auto-restart via supervisor; no crash = goal

4.2 Memory Budget Breakdown

Component Budget Rationale
Node arena (1 M nodes × ~200 B) ~200 MB BuildNode struct; deps Vec inline
Adjacency + rdep vecs ~160 MB 10 M edges × 16 B (from+to)
Engine NodeState map ~64 MB 1 M nodes × 64 B per state
Digest cache (fingerprint) ~100 MB (path, mtime, size) → 32 B digest; LRU
Action cache index (in-memory) ~50 MB 500 k entries × 100 B
Scheduler ready queue ~10 MB Bounded; 100 k pending actions max
ibg-persist mmap window ~256 MB (configurable) OS manages eviction
Total ceiling ≤ 2 GB Hard limit; OOM → IbgError::ResourceExhausted

4.3 Throughput + Latency Targets

Scenario Metric Target
Null build (0 dirty nodes) Wall time ≤ 50 ms
1 % dirty (10 k of 1 M nodes) Incremental eval + schedule ≤ 500 ms excl. exec
Full clean build (1 M nodes, all cached) Cache hit + materialize ≤ 30 s (I/O bound)
Fingerprint throughput Bytes/sec ≥ 500 MB/s (SIMD blake3)
Scheduler throughput Actions dispatched/sec ≥ 50 k/s
Cache hit rate target (warm build) % ≥ 95 %

4.4 Concurrency Constraints

  • Worker threads: min(max_jobs, num_cpus) rayon threads for CPU-bound graph work; tokio runtime with max_blocking_threads = 128 for I/O.
  • No global lock on DAG after construction phase; per-node Mutex<NodeState> or atomic CAS.
  • File descriptor limit: inotify watches capped at min(rlimit_nofile/2, 8192); excess paths fall back to polling.
  • NUMA: all arenas allocated on local node; no explicit NUMA binding (OS first-touch policy sufficient for current scope).

4.5 Storage Constraints

Store Max size Eviction
Local CAS Configurable; default 10 GB LRU-size; sweep on GC trigger
Action cache index 500 k entries LRU-count; configurable
ibg-persist DB ≤ 2 GB (1 M nodes × ~2 KB state) Schema migration drops orphan entries
Remote cache Unbounded (provider-managed) Outside scope

4.6 Failure Budget

Failure Recovery SLO impact
Corrupt ibg-persist Full re-eval One extra full build; ≤ 10 s overhead
Remote cache unavailable Fall back to local exec No correctness impact; latency +exec time
inotify queue overflow Full workspace rescan One extra fingerprint pass; ≤ 5 s
Exec OOM IbgError::ExecFailed; node marked Failed Propagates to dependents; user notified
Undeclared input detected Action fails loudly Correctness preserved; build fails fast
Non-deterministic action IbgError::NonDeterministic if repro-check enabled Build fails; cache NOT poisoned

5. Required Reading

Reading discipline: Study reference implementations before writing corresponding ibg crates; papers before design decisions; specs before protocol code. Annotate divergences from references with explicit ADR entries (§9).

5.1 Books

# Title / Author Chapters / Sections Concepts to Extract
B1 The Rust Programming Language — Klabnik & Nichols (2nd ed.) Ch 15 (smart pointers), Ch 16 (concurrency), Ch 19 (advanced types) Arc/Mutex patterns for NodeState; unsafe for mmap; type-state for Dirty/Verified
B2 Rust Atomics and Locks — Mara Bos (O'Reilly 2023) Ch 2 (atomics), Ch 4 (spin locks), Ch 9 (lock-free) Revision counter (AtomicU64); work-stealing queue implementation; backpressure channels
B3 Programming Rust — Blandy, Orendorff, Tindall (2nd ed.) Ch 2 (types), Ch 21 (async), Ch 22 (unsafe) Zero-copy digest slices; async executor integration for ibg-sched; mmap safety

B5 The Art of Multiprocessor Programming — Herlihy & Shavit (2nd ed.) Ch 10 (queues), Ch 16 (work stealing) Chase-Lev deque for ibg-sched; ABA avoidance; linearizability of ready-queue ops

Reading priority for books: B1–B3 before any Rust code; B4 + B6 before ibg-persist; B5 before ibg-sched; B7 before ibg-remote; B8 before ibg-telemetry performance work.

Key concept extraction per book:

  • B1: ownership + borrow checker for NodeState mutation; interior mutability (RwLock<NodeState>) pattern.
  • B2: SeqCst vs Acquire/Release for Revision counter; memory ordering in work-stealing deque.
  • B3: async fn in ibg-engine eval; unsafe for CAS mmap; zero-copy CasObject slices via Bytes.
  • B4: WAL + checkpoint pattern maps directly to ibg-persist design; content-addressed storage chapter.
  • B5: Chase-Lev deque complexity; ABA problem in lock-free ready-queue.
  • B6: LMDB MVCC vs RocksDB LSM — evaluated for ibg-persist backend (see ADR-08).
  • B7: Remote cache trust model; cache poisoning mitigation; hermetic sandbox rationale.
  • B8: flamegraph + perf for critical-path profiling; inotify vs polling latency benchmarks.

Reading priority for papers: P1 (taxonomy) → P3 (Salsa) → P2 (Shake) before ibg-engine design; P6 (Bazel) → P4 (Adapton) → P7 (DICE) for cache + invalidation model; P12 + P11 for ibg-sched; P14 before ibg-fingerprint; P15 before ibg-persist WAL design.

Key extraction per paper:

  • P1: Table 1 (rebuilder × scheduler matrix) → ibg-engine is "Suspending" rebuilder + dependency-aware scheduler; Theorem 1 (correctness) + Theorem 2 (minimality) → acceptance criteria for correctness test suite.
  • P2: Rules monad in Shake → ibg-graph Edge{kind:Dynamic}; EqualCost → early cutoff trigger; Shake DB format → ibg-persist key-value schema inspiration.
  • P3: salsa::query_group! macro → ibg-engine query trait pattern; Revision type directly adopted; red/green propagation algorithm studied line-by-line.
  • P4: DCG (Demanded Computation Graph) → conceptual model for ibg-engine + ibg-graph joint responsibility; change propagation theorem → early cutoff soundness.
  • P6: ActionKey computation from Bazel → adopt input Merkle digest; sandbox via linux-sandbox binary → ibg-sandbox seccomp approach; remote cache hit rate target ≥ 85%.
  • P14: BLAKE3 tree hashing → ibg-fingerprint parallel directory digest; keyed mode → domain-separate ActionKey from CAS Digest.

6. Design Principles

6.1 Correctness Before Speed

  • Statement: A stale output is a build-system failure; incorrect incremental results are worse than slow ones.
  • Tradeoff: Accept higher re-computation cost when correctness of ActionKey or declared-input coverage is uncertain.
  • Operational: Cache hits are served ONLY when ActionKey matches exactly; partial/fuzzy matches are forbidden.
  • Violation example: Using mtime-only change detection (no Digest) — clock skew or touch(1) causes under-build; real outputs become stale while system claims Clean.
  • Enforcement: ibg-fingerprint always falls back from stat fast-path to full content hash when mtime is ambiguous; ActionKey is always a Digest of inputs, never of timestamps.

6.2 Declare Everything, Trust Nothing

  • Statement: Every input and output of every action must be explicitly declared; any undeclared input renders the build non-hermetic and the cache unsound.
  • Tradeoff: More verbose action definitions; glob nodes handle dynamic file sets; discovery overhead at graph-construction time.
  • Operational: ibg-sandbox detects undeclared inputs at exec time; build fails loudly rather than silently caching a polluted output.
  • Violation example: An action reads a config file not listed in inputs — ActionKey does not change when config changes → stale cached output served to dependents → under-build.
  • Enforcement: Sandbox open()/openat() interception; CacheEntry stored only after sandbox confirms all reads were declared.

6.3 Content-Addressed Immutability

  • Statement: Artifacts are identified by Digest; the same Digest always refers to the same bytes; CAS objects are write-once, never mutated.
  • Tradeoff: Storage growth requires explicit GC; deduplication is automatic.
  • Operational: CAS collision (digest reuse for different bytes) is a catastrophic correctness bug — treated as hardware/software fault; triggers cache invalidation + alert.
  • Violation example: Mutable output paths (overwrite in place without CAS) → concurrent readers see partial writes → nondeterministic failures.
  • Enforcement: ibg-cache write path: compute Digest of output bytes BEFORE writing to CAS; verify on read (optional paranoia mode).

6.4 Early Cutoff Maximizes Minimality

  • Statement: If a changed input's action produces an OUTPUT digest identical to the previous run, do NOT propagate dirtiness to dependents.
  • Tradeoff: Requires storing previous output Digest (NodeState.output_digest); extra compare step; worth it for large fan-out graphs.
  • Operational: Critically important for generated headers, proto-compiled artifacts, formatting-only changes — cuts large subgraph re-evaluation to zero.
  • Violation example: Propagating dirtiness past an unchanged output — entire downstream subgraph rebuilds on a whitespace-only change to a header.
  • Enforcement: ibg-engine eval loop: after exec or cache-hit, compare new output_digest to stored; if equal, update last_verified without incrementing last_changed.

6.5 Demand-Driven (Pull) Evaluation

  • Statement: Nodes are evaluated only when demanded by a target reachable from BuildRequest; unrequested nodes are never touched.
  • Tradeoff: Requires explicit traversal from targets; graph may have many unreachable nodes in large monorepos.
  • Operational: Pull semantics enable partial builds and watch-mode interactive builds; push (reactive) would over-evaluate on every file event.
  • Violation example: Push-based invalidation that eagerly re-evaluates all transitive dependents on every file change — kills interactivity in large repos with hot watch loops.
  • Enforcement: ibg-engine traversal starts at BuildRequest.targets; never evaluates a node not reachable from the current demand set.

6.6 Monotonic, Snapshot-Consistent Revisions

  • Statement: Revision(u64) is globally monotonic; every evaluation epoch has a fixed Revision; NodeState.last_verified ≤ current Revision at all times.
  • Tradeoff: Single global counter is a potential bottleneck in parallel eval — mitigated by only incrementing on file events (low frequency), not per action.
  • Operational: Snapshots allow consistent reads: a node's Clean/Dirty determination is made relative to a fixed Revision, preventing TOCTOU within a build.
  • Violation example: Using wall-clock time as version counter — NTP adjustments cause non-monotonic "Revisions" → last_verified > current Revision → incorrect Clean claim.
  • Enforcement: AtomicU64 fetch_add for Revision; ibg-persist stores last snapshot Revision; recovery rejects state with future Revision.

6.7 Crash-Only / Restart-Safe Persistence

  • Statement: ibg-persist must tolerate process kill at any point and produce either fully committed or fully rolled-back state — never a partial update.
  • Tradeoff: WAL + checksum overhead (~5–10% write amplification); necessary to avoid incorrect incremental results after crash.
  • Operational: On startup: verify checksum of last checkpoint; if corrupt, fall back to full re-evaluation (safe, not incorrect).
  • Violation example: Persisting NodeState in-place without WAL — partial write leaves last_verified advanced beyond actual content hash → system believes node Clean when it is not → under-build.
  • Enforcement: Write to temp file + fsync + atomic rename; checksum every persisted record; startup validation in ibg-persist::open().

6.8 Backpressure Everywhere

  • Statement: Every producer-consumer boundary (graph eval → scheduler → executor → cache write) must have bounded queues with backpressure; unbounded queues are forbidden.
  • Tradeoff: Adds complexity at boundaries; prevents OOM on large fan-out graphs; may throttle parallelism if downstream is slow.
  • Operational: ibg-sched ready-queue has configurable capacity; ibg-exec job-slot semaphore; ibg-cache write channel bounded; exceeded capacity → caller blocks (not drops).
  • Violation example: Unbounded ready-queue on a 100k-node monorepo fan-out — all nodes enqueued simultaneously → 2GB heap → OOM before any action runs.
  • Enforcement: All async channels in ibg-sched use tokio::sync::mpsc with explicit capacity; semaphore for exec concurrency.

6.9 Observability by Default

  • Statement: Every cache lookup, action execution, scheduling decision, and invalidation must emit telemetry (trace span + metric counter/histogram) unconditionally.
  • Tradeoff: Small overhead (~1–2 µs per span); never disabled in production because build performance debugging without traces is blind.
  • Operational: ibg-telemetry taps: cache hit/miss rate; critical-path duration; recompute count; dirty-node count per invocation; action exec duration p50/p95/p99.
  • Violation example: Telemetry behind a --debug flag — oncall cannot diagnose "why did this build take 3x longer" without reproducing locally.
  • Enforcement: ibg-telemetry initialized before ibg-engine starts; spans are always-on; OTLP export is configurable but span creation is not.

6.10 Hermeticity as Correctness Invariant

  • Statement: An action's outputs are fully determined by its ActionKey; any environmental input not captured in ActionKey is a hermeticity violation and a bug.
  • Tradeoff: Strict hermeticity requires clamping timestamps, controlling env vars, filtering PATH — developer experience friction; worthwhile because cache unsoundness is worse.
  • Operational: ibg-sandbox enforces: read-only bind mounts for declared inputs; writable scratch only for declared output paths; blocked network by default; fixed SOURCE_DATE_EPOCH.
  • Violation example: Build action reads /etc/hostname (not declared) → different outputs on CI vs dev machine → same ActionKey, different CAS objects → cache corrupts results.
  • Enforcement: Sandbox intercepts all file reads; any read outside declared inputs → action fails with UndeclaredInputError, never cached.

6.11 Cycle Freedom is a Hard Invariant

  • Statement: The build graph is always a DAG; a dynamic dependency that would create a cycle is detected immediately and returns IbgError::CycleDetected — never silently deadlocks.
  • Tradeoff: Cycle detection overhead O(V+E) at graph construction; incremental DFS on dynamic dep addition.
  • Operational: Static cycles caught at graph construction; dynamic cycles caught during demand-driven eval when a node discovers it depends on an ancestor.
  • Violation example: Undetected cycle → two work-stealing threads each waiting for the other's node → scheduler deadlock with no progress → build hangs indefinitely.
  • Enforcement: ibg-graph maintains topological sort + color state; ibg-engine maintains in-progress set per evaluation stack; DynamicDep addition triggers cycle check.

6.12 Minimal Coupling Between Crates

  • Statement: ibg-core defines all shared types; crates depend only on ibg-core (and direct functional dependencies); no circular crate dependencies; ibg-engine does not import ibg-exec directly (only via trait).
  • Tradeoff: Trait-object dispatch (~3 ns) on execution boundary; enables mock executors in tests; enables alternative backends.
  • Operational: ibg-engine::Executor trait; ibg-sched::Scheduler trait; ibg-cache::ActionCache trait — all defined in respective crates, implemented by concrete types.
  • Violation example: ibg-engine imports ibg-exec directly → cannot unit-test incremental logic without spawning real processes → 100x slower tests.
  • Enforcement: Cargo.toml dependency graph enforced in CI; cargo deny checks for unexpected transitive deps.

6.13 Determinism and Reproducibility as First-Class Goals

  • Statement: Given the same declared inputs and ActionKey, an action must produce bit-identical outputs; non-determinism is detected (reproducibility check) and reported.
  • Tradeoff: Reproducibility double-execution is expensive; done on sampling basis or CI-only; detection is more important than prevention.
  • Operational: ibg-sandbox can re-run an action twice and compare output Digests; mismatch → NonDeterministicAction metric + log; cached entry marked suspect.
  • Violation example: Linking order non-determinism in a C++ build → same ActionKey → different output Digest on re-run → remote cache poisoning across machines.
  • Enforcement: ibg-sandbox::reproducibility_check() optional flag; ibg-telemetry nondeterminism_detected counter; cache marks entry with NonDeterministic flag.

6.14 Separation of Graph Model from Evaluation Policy

  • Statement: ibg-graph owns the DAG structure and topology; ibg-engine owns when and whether to re-evaluate; ibg-sched owns parallelism and ordering — these are three separate concerns.
  • Tradeoff: More indirection; clear boundaries prevent "build policy creep" into the graph model.
  • Operational: Graph mutations (add/remove edge) are graph-layer operations; invalidation is engine-layer; scheduling is sched-layer; each can be replaced independently.
  • Violation example: Scheduling logic embedded in graph traversal → cannot swap FIFO for critical-path scheduling without rewriting the graph module.
  • Enforcement: crate boundaries enforce separation; ibg-graph has no dependency on ibg-engine or ibg-sched.

6.15 Fail Loudly, Never Silently Wrong

  • Statement: Ambiguity (hash collision, corrupt state, undeclared input, cycle) always results in a loud error or fallback to full re-evaluation — never a silent incorrect result.
  • Tradeoff: More user-visible errors; some false-positive full rebuilds on DB corruption; correct behavior is non-negotiable.
  • Operational: IbgError variants are exhaustive; all error paths log structured events; ibg-telemetry error_count counter per error kind.
  • Violation example: Silently ignoring a corrupt WAL record and using last known state → stale NodeState used → incorrect incremental result served to developer.
  • Enforcement: All ? propagation in ibg-persist hits the "fallback to full eval" path, not "use corrupt state" path.

7. System Invariants

7.1 No Under-Build (Correctness)

  • Statement: If any transitive declared input of a demanded target changed (by Digest), the target is either rebuilt or proven unaffected by early cutoff; a stale output is never served as current.
  • Why: Foundation of build system correctness; developer trust depends on "if it built, it's correct."
  • Where enforced: ibg-engine eval loop; ibg-fingerprint digest comparison; ibg-cache hit validation (ActionKey must match recomputed key).
  • Violation detection: Audit mode: recompute all input digests post-build and compare to NodeState; mismatch → under_build_detected metric.
  • Telemetry: ibg.correctness.stale_output_served counter (must always be 0); post-build audit failures logged as CRITICAL.
  • Recovery: Invalidate the specific node's NodeState; force re-evaluation on next build; alert.
  • Debug methodology: Replay eval log; compare NodeState.last_changed to expected Revision; verify ActionKey components one by one.

7.2 No Over-Build (Minimality)

  • Statement: A node re-executes only if its recomputed ActionKey differs from the cached ActionKey AND no cache hit exists; unchanged subgraphs do exactly zero work (no stat, no hash, no exec).
  • Why: Performance contract; over-build in large monorepos can multiply build times 10–100x.
  • Where enforced: ibg-engine: check last_verified against current Revision before triggering re-eval; early cutoff after each action.
  • Violation detection: Count nodes re-executed per build; compare to changed-file set; ibg-telemetry unnecessary_recompute_count.
  • Telemetry: ibg.minimality.unnecessary_recomputes histogram; should be 0 for unchanged targets.
  • Recovery: Investigate ActionKey computation for spurious non-determinism; check env var leakage into ActionKey.
  • Debug methodology: Enable --explain flag; engine logs each node's dirty reason; trace ActionKey component diffs.

7.3 Early-Cutoff Soundness

  • Statement: A node marked verified at Revision R because its output Digest is unchanged is correct: its dependents may use its outputs without re-evaluation, and this is observationally equivalent to re-running.
  • Why: Correctness of the early-cutoff optimization; if unsound, dependents use wrong outputs.
  • Where enforced: ibg-engine: output Digest comparison is always a full 32-byte compare; NodeState.output_digest is only set after confirmed exec or cache-hit with matching ActionKey.
  • Violation detection: Test: modify input, run, verify output unchanged, verify dependents not re-run; then modify input to produce different output, verify dependents DO re-run.
  • Telemetry: ibg.engine.early_cutoff_applied counter; ibg.engine.cutoff_false_negative (should be 0).
  • Recovery: If cutoff_false_negative detected, disable early-cutoff and rebuild; file correctness bug.
  • Debug methodology: Compare NodeState.output_digest before/after suspected incorrect cutoff; verify CAS object at that Digest is actually current.

7.4 ActionKey Uniqueness (Cache Soundness)

  • Statement: Two distinct declared-input sets, commands, or envs must produce distinct ActionKeys with overwhelming probability (BLAKE3 collision resistance, 2^128 security).
  • Why: Cache hit on wrong ActionKey → incorrect outputs served; cache poisoning.
  • Where enforced: ibg-fingerprint ActionKey computation: blake3(command || sort(input_digests) || env_canon || tool_digest); canonical encoding (sorted, length-prefixed) prevents extension attacks.
  • Violation detection: Log all ActionKey computations in debug mode; compare computed key to stored key on cache hit.
  • Telemetry: ibg.cache.key_collision_detected counter (must always be 0; if non-zero → critical alert).
  • Recovery: Treat as hardware fault (hash collision at 2^-128 probability) or canonicalization bug; invalidate cache; full rebuild.
  • Debug methodology: Decompose ActionKey from constituent fields; verify canonical encoding; check for missing inputs or env var inclusion.

7.5 Hermeticity (Undeclared Input Freedom)

  • Statement: No action reads a file not listed in its declared inputs; any such read is detected at exec time and causes action failure — the result is NEVER cached.
  • Why: Undeclared input = ActionKey does not capture all dependencies = cache unsoundness = under-build.
  • Where enforced: ibg-sandbox on every exec; read interception via seccomp-bpf + ptrace or Linux namespaces.
  • Violation detection: Sandbox emits UndeclaredInputError with the offending path; ibg.sandbox.undeclared_input_count metric.
  • Telemetry: Per-action undeclared_input events; aggregated by action kind; alert if count > 0 post-sandbox enforcement.
  • Recovery: Build fails with actionable error listing undeclared paths; developer adds them to declared inputs.
  • Debug methodology: Run with --sandbox-verbose; inspect strace/seccomp log for offending open() calls; cross-reference with Action.inputs.

7.6 Cycle Freedom

  • Statement: The build graph is a DAG at all times; no static cycle may be added; no dynamic dependency may create a cycle; violation → IbgError::CycleDetected, never deadlock.
  • Why: A cycle causes the scheduler to deadlock waiting for mutually dependent nodes.
  • Where enforced: ibg-graph: DFS cycle check on static edge addition; ibg-engine: in-progress ancestor set checked on every dynamic dep discovery.
  • Violation detection: ibg-graph::add_edge() returns Err(CycleDetected) immediately; ibg-engine::add_dynamic_dep() checks ancestor chain.
  • Telemetry: ibg.graph.cycle_detected_count; logs affected nodes and discovery path.
  • Recovery: Build fails; user must fix BUILD file / action declarations.
  • Debug methodology: Print cycle path from error payload; visualize with ibg-cli graph --dot | dot -Tsvg.

7.7 Revision Monotonicity

  • Statement: The global Revision counter is strictly monotonically increasing; NodeState.last_verified and last_changed are always ≤ the current Revision.
  • Why: Non-monotonic Revisions make Clean/Dirty determination unreliable; can cause both over- and under-builds.
  • Where enforced: ibg-engine: AtomicU64::fetch_add(1, Ordering::SeqCst) only; ibg-persist: startup validates stored Revision ≤ u64::MAX - SAFETY_MARGIN.
  • Violation detection: Startup: reject persisted state with Revision > current; assert in debug builds that last_verified ≤ current.
  • Telemetry: ibg.persist.revision_skew_detected (should always be 0).
  • Recovery: On revision skew: wipe persisted state, start from Revision 0, full re-evaluation.
  • Debug methodology: Inspect persisted Revision in DB; compare to in-memory counter; investigate if multiple processes share state.

7.8 Crash-Consistent Persistence

  • Statement: ibg-persist writes are atomic from the consumer's perspective: a partial write (due to process kill or power loss) is always detectable and results in fallback to full re-evaluation, never incorrect incremental results.
  • Why: Corrupt NodeState claiming Clean for a Dirty node is a silent under-build — worse than a full rebuild.
  • Where enforced: ibg-persist: WAL + checksum per record; atomic rename on checkpoint; startup validation.
  • Violation detection: Checksum mismatch on record read → log CorruptState event → discard entire checkpoint → full eval fallback.
  • Telemetry: ibg.persist.corrupt_state_detected; ibg.persist.fallback_full_eval_count.
  • Recovery: Full re-evaluation is correct (conservative); after successful build, state is re-persisted cleanly.
  • Debug methodology: Run ibg-cli db verify; inspect WAL for partial records; check filesystem for incomplete renames.

7.9 CAS Object Immutability

  • Statement: A CasObject identified by Digest is write-once; its bytes never change after initial write; reads always return the same bytes for the same Digest.
  • Why: Mutable CAS objects break cache soundness; concurrent readers see inconsistent content.
  • Where enforced: ibg-cache: write path uses tmp-write + rename; read path optionally re-verifies Digest.
  • Violation detection: Paranoia mode: re-hash CAS object on read; compare to key; ibg.cache.cas_integrity_failure metric.
  • Telemetry: ibg.cache.cas_write_collisions (should be 0 given blake3 collision resistance).
  • Recovery: On integrity failure: evict corrupted object; re-execute action; alert (likely filesystem or hardware fault).
  • Debug methodology: ibg-cli cache verify <digest>; compare stored digest to blake3sum of object bytes.

7.10 Scheduler Forward Progress

  • Statement: The work-stealing scheduler makes forward progress as long as any ready node exists; no livelock, no starvation, no deadlock under DAG invariants.
  • Why: Build hangs are equivalent to failures from the developer's perspective; hard to diagnose.
  • Where enforced: ibg-sched: ready-queue fed only when all static deps are verified; dynamic dep addition re-enqueues; job-slot semaphore with timeout.
  • Violation detection: Watchdog: if no action completes in T seconds (configurable, default 60s) and ready-queue is non-empty → log stuck_scheduler event.
  • Telemetry: ibg.sched.stuck_scheduler_count; ibg.sched.ready_queue_depth gauge; ibg.sched.worker_idle_time histogram.
  • Recovery: Log all in-flight nodes and their deps; kill and restart build; file bug with trace.
  • Debug methodology: ibg-cli sched status dumps ready-queue and in-flight nodes; cross-reference with graph cycle check.

7.11 Dynamic Dependency Consistency

  • Statement: A dynamic dep discovered during evaluation of node A is recorded at the Revision of that evaluation; if the discovered dep's output changes in a subsequent Revision, A is re-evaluated.
  • Why: Dynamic deps that are not tracked across Revisions cause under-build when discovered deps change.
  • Where enforced: ibg-engine: DynamicDep{discovered_at: Revision} stored in NodeState; on next eval, all dynamic deps re-checked against their last_changed.
  • Violation detection: Integration test: node A discovers dep B dynamically; change B; verify A re-evaluates.
  • Telemetry: ibg.engine.dynamic_dep_invalidation_count; ibg.engine.dynamic_dep_total.
  • Recovery: If missed dynamic dep: full invalidation of affected node; investigate engine eval loop.
  • Debug methodology: Enable dynamic-dep trace log; verify DynamicDep records in persisted state match expected.

7.12 Glob Node Stability

  • Statement: A Glob node's output (set of matching file paths) is a pure function of the filesystem state at the current Revision; its Digest changes if and only if the matching set or file contents change.
  • Why: Glob nodes feed many actions in monorepos; spurious glob invalidations cause massive over-build.
  • Where enforced: ibg-graph GlobNode: re-expands on demand; compares sorted path-list digest to stored digest; uses ibg-watch events to detect new/deleted files in glob scope.
  • Violation detection: Test: glob over directory, add file outside glob pattern, verify glob digest unchanged. Add file inside pattern, verify digest changes.
  • Telemetry: ibg.graph.glob_expansion_count; ibg.graph.glob_invalidation_count.
  • Recovery: On incorrect glob expansion (e.g., watch event missed): ibg-watch polling fallback triggers re-expansion.
  • Debug methodology: ibg-cli graph glob-expand <pattern> shows current expansion; compare to persisted.

7.13 Resource Bound Enforcement

  • Statement: Job-slot count (exec parallelism), ready-queue depth, cache write queue, and in-memory NodeState count are all bounded by configured limits; no resource is unbounded.
  • Why: Unbounded resource usage causes OOM or file-descriptor exhaustion on large graphs, leading to failed builds or OS instability.
  • Where enforced: ibg-sched: Semaphore(job_slots); ibg-cache: bounded write channel; ibg-engine: optional max-in-memory-nodes with LRU eviction to ibg-persist.
  • Violation detection: ibg.resource.oom_detected; process RSS monitoring; fd count monitoring.
  • Telemetry: ibg.sched.job_slot_utilization; ibg.cache.write_queue_depth; ibg.engine.in_memory_node_count.
  • Recovery: Reduce parallelism; increase cache write throughput; enable NodeState eviction to ibg-persist.
  • Debug methodology: ibg-cli perf resources shows current utilization vs limits; heap profile if OOM suspected.

7.14 Remote Cache Safety (No Poisoning)

  • Statement: A remote cache hit is accepted only if the locally recomputed ActionKey matches the remote entry's key; the result is never accepted on partial key match or timeout degradation.
  • Why: Remote cache poisoning (wrong result served) is a supply-chain correctness failure affecting all developers sharing the cache.
  • Where enforced: ibg-remote: compare locally computed ActionKey to remote entry key before materializing outputs; reject on mismatch.
  • Violation detection: ibg.remote.key_mismatch_on_fetch metric; any non-zero value triggers cache invalidation and alert.
  • Telemetry: ibg.remote.cache_hit_rate; ibg.remote.poisoning_attempt_detected; ibg.remote.fetch_error_count.
  • Recovery: Discard remote result; execute locally; log incident; investigate remote cache integrity.
  • Debug methodology: Enable --remote-verbose; log full ActionKey on every fetch; compare to remote-returned key in response metadata.

7.15 Schema Version Enforcement

  • Statement: ibg-persist rejects state files whose schema version is unknown or incompatible; it never attempts to deserialize future or corrupted schema versions.
  • Why: Misinterpreting persisted NodeState fields across schema versions causes incorrect Revision values or wrong Digest comparisons → silent under-build.
  • Where enforced: ibg-persist: version header read on open(); CURRENT_SCHEMA_VERSION constant; reject if stored_version > CURRENT or if migration chain missing.
  • Violation detection: Startup log: schema_version_mismatch event; ibg.persist.schema_migration_count metric.
  • Recovery: On unknown version: wipe state, full re-evaluation. On known older version: run migration. On newer version: error (user must upgrade ibg binary).
  • Debug methodology: ibg-cli db inspect --header shows stored schema version; compare to binary's expected version.
